    FINITE-TIME BLOW-UP FOR THE NAVIER–STOKES EQUATIONS

                           LEVENT ALPÖGE AND TRISTAN BUCKMASTER


        Abstract. We prove that there exist smooth, compactly supported, divergence-free initial
        data on R3 , and smooth divergence-free initial data on the torus T3 , for which the solutions
        of the three-dimensional Navier–Stokes equations without force blow up in finite time. This
        disproves the statements (A) and (B) of the Millennium Prize problem as formulated by
        Fefferman, and hence proves the statements (C) and (D). These solutions converge in self-
        similar variables to a backward discretely self-similar solution and blow up at its rate, which
        is (T − t)−1/4 in Ḣ 1 . The discretely self-similar solution is obtained from a certificate: an
        approximate solution that satisfies explicit inequalities. The certificate will be released soon;
        its hash is 3f7a335b84b6c7631742cacc5343dfcd11cf72f5221ff79b2b9268790b4b22c5.

                                             1. Introduction
  We consider the incompressible Navier–Stokes equations in R3 without external force,
(1.1)                         ∂t u + (u · ∇)u = ∆u − ∇p,              div u = 0.
Here u is the velocity, p is the pressure, and the viscosity is normalized to one: if (u, p)
solves (1.1), then (ν u(νt, x), ν 2 p(νt, x)) solves the equations with viscosity ν > 0, so that the
results of this paper hold, after this change of variables, for every viscosity. Whether smooth
solutions of (1.1) with finite energy, or smooth solutions on the torus T3 = R3 /Z3 , can develop
singularities in finite time is the regularity problem for the Navier–Stokes equations [53].
   Leray [70, §20] proposed to look for√singular solutions of (1.1) among the backward self-
similar ones, u(t, x) = (T − t)−1/2 U (x/ T − t). Nečas, Růžička and Šverák [76] and Tsai [86]
proved that there is no such solution other than zero, under natural integrability conditions
on the profile U . The next candidate is a solution that is invariant under the scaling of
the equations for one scaling factor only, possibly after a rotation: a discretely self-similar

                                                       1
2                          LEVENT ALPÖGE AND TRISTAN BUCKMASTER

solution. Whether √   a nonzero solution of this kind exists backward in time, with the bound
|u(t, x)| ≤ C (|x| + T − t)−1 , is the subject of [13, Open Problem 5.1].
   In this paper we prove that smooth solutions of (1.1) can blow up in finite time: there exist
smooth, compactly supported, divergence-free data on R3 , and smooth divergence-free data on
T3 , whose solutions are smooth up to a finite time, at which they blow up. In the formulation of
the regularity problem by Fefferman [53], this disproves his statements (A) and (B) and hence
proves his statements (C) and (D). The proof has two parts. The first is Theorem 1.1: one
smooth discretely self-similar solution of the kind above implies this blow-up. Its proof shows
that such a solution is stable in Ḣ 1 up to finitely many directions, whatever the spectrum of
its linearization is. The second is Theorem 7.1: such a solution exists near an approximate one
that satisfies explicit inequalities, which we call a certificate.
1.1. Main results. Let S > 0, and let R ∈ O(3) be a linear isometry of R3 . A profile with
period S and isometry R is a pair (W, q) of smooth functions W : R × R3 → R3 , not identically
zero, and q : R × R3 → R such that, on R × R3 ,

In the terminology of Bradshaw and Tsai [13], u∗T is a backward discretely self-similar solution
when R = I and a backward rotated discretely self-similar solution when R is a rotation; when
R is not a rotation, R2 is a rotation, and u∗T is of this kind for the period 2S. The first of the
two bounds is the bound of a Type I singularity at the point (T, 0), and the second follows
from it (Remark 1.2).
   Let 0 < T ≤ ∞. A smooth solution of (1.1) on [0, T ) × R3 with datum u0 is a pair (u, p)
of functions that are smooth on [0, T ) × R3 , up to the boundary, and satisfy (1.1) there, with
u(0, ·) = u0 ; we also call the velocity u a smooth solution. For T < ∞ the smooth solutions
on [0, T ] × R3 are defined in the same way. We write u(t) = u(t, ·), and we say that u has
finite energy if ku(t)kL2 is bounded in t. We call a function on R3 periodic if it has period
1 in each coordinate, and we identify the functions on T3 with the periodic functions on R3 .
Thus a smooth solution on [0, T ) × T3 , or on [0, T ] × T3 , is a smooth solution on [0, T ) × R3 ,
or on [0, T ] × R3 , for which u(t) and p(t) are periodic. We write Br for the open ball of radius
r about the origin of R3 and kukH 1 (T3 ) = kukH 1 ([0,1]3 ) for the norm of H 1 on the torus. Our
main result is the following theorem.

smooth solution u of (1.1) on [0, 1) × R3 with datum u0 and finite energy which blows up at
time 1: there exist κ ∈ (0, 41 ) and constants 0 < c ≤ C such that

   The rate (T − t)−1/4 is that of u∗T : we have k∇u∗T (t)kL2 = (T − t)−1/4 k∇W (s, ·)kL2 , and
k∇W (s, ·)kL2 is bounded above and below, uniformly in s (Lemma 4.2). This is also the
rate of the lower bound of Leray [70, §21] for k∇u(t)kL2 when a solution becomes singular
at time T . In self-similar variables, u(t, x) = (1 − t)−1/2 U (s, y), the second bound of (1.6)
reads k∇(U (s, ·) − W (s, ·))kL2 ≤ C e−κs , so that u converges to the profile. By (1.7) the

(Subsection 1.3).
   These properties are not special to the datum u0 that we construct. There exist ε0 > 0
and a finite-dimensional space E of smooth, compactly supported, divergence-free fields such
that every divergence-free w0 ∈ Cc∞ (R3 ; R3 ) with k∇(w0 − W (0, ·))kL2 ≤ ε0 becomes, after the
addition of an element of E, the datum of a smooth solution of finite energy on [0, 1) × R3
which satisfies (1.6) (Proposition 2.1 and Lemma 2.3); the datum u0 is of this form. In this
sense the blow-up is stable in finite codimension.
Remark 1.2. The bound on ∇W in (1.3) is a consequence of the bound on W and of the first two
equations of (1.2), with a constant that depends only on CW , and so is the bound |∇2 W (s, y)| ≤
C (1 + |y|)−3 . This follows, as in [79, Lemma 9.2 and Corollary 9.3], from the interior estimate
for bounded smooth solutions of the Navier–Stokes equations [79, Lemma 9.1], which requires
                                                         3
no bound on the pressure,
                1
                           √ applied, for t < T and x ∈ R , on the backward parabolic cylinder
of radius r = 2 max{|x|, T − t} about the point (t, x), on which the field (1.4) is bounded by
CW /r. Thus the hypothesis of Theorem 1.1 is that of a nonzero smooth solution of (1.2) with
the first bound of (1.3).
   Theorem 1.1 assumes a profile. In Section 7 we obtain one from a certificate. A certificate,
defined in Subsection 7.3, consists of a family of approximate profiles with their periods, and of
further fields and numbers. The approximate profiles are approximate solutions of the first two
equations of (1.2), periodic in s, and depend on finitely many parameters. The conditions of a
certificate are of two kinds. Some are estimates that are to hold for every perturbation of the
approximate profiles, among them an a priori estimate for the linearized equation. The others
are explicit inequalities between norms of these fields and between these numbers, of the kind
that a computation with rigorous bounds for its errors can verify. We prove that a certificate
gives a profile whose isometry is the identity, close to one of the approximate profiles, with its
period and with the symmetries that the certificate prescribes for these profiles (Theorem 7.1),
and hence, by Theorem 1.1, blow-up from smooth, compactly supported data on R3 and from
4                          LEVENT ALPÖGE AND TRISTAN BUCKMASTER

smooth data on T3 (Corollary 7.2). The certificate from which we obtain the profile is not
printed in this paper: it will be released soon, and its hash is 3f7a335b84b6c7631742cacc
5343dfcd11cf72f5221ff79b2b9268790b4b22c5. It consists of data of the form described in
Appendix B.1 that satisfy the conditions of Appendix B. These are conditions on finitely many
functions and numbers, from which the estimates for every perturbation are derived in that
appendix; two steps of the derivation are proved in the formalization (Subsection 1.5) and not
in this paper. That the certificate satisfies the conditions is verified by a rigorous computation,
involving interval arithmetic, which is not part of the formalization. The interval arithmetic
has 128 bits of precision and uses the Arb library [66], through FLINT.
   The approximate profiles of this certificate are formed from one field and from further
fields computed from it. This field was found by a numerical computation in floating-point
arithmetic: Newton’s method on an adaptive discretization, started from a state that was
reached by continuation; we describe the computation in Appendix C. Figure 1 shows this
field, which we denote by W̄ . It is not axisymmetric. It is invariant under the rotation by
2π/19 about the y3 axis and under the rotation by π about the y1 axis; it is periodic in s,
with the computed period S̄ ≈ 0.36; and an advance of s by S̄/2 rotates it by the angle π/19
about the y3 axis. The norm of its gradient in L2 is of order 104 at every time. The certificate
prescribes these symmetries, so that the profile W that Theorem 7.1 gives for it has them as
well (Remark 7.3): it is a profile with the isometry I and a period S, and also with the rotation
by π/19 about the y3 axis and the period S/2. In the terminology recalled above, its field u∗T
is thus both a backward discretely self-similar and a backward rotated discretely self-similar
solution. By (7.38) and the conditions of Appendix B it differs from one of the approximate
profiles of the certificate by less than 0.017 in Ḣ 1 at every time.
   In the certificate the symmetries restrict the perturbations for which the estimates are
required (Section 7). Classes of fields with a discrete rotational symmetry have been used for
the Euler equations. Elgindi and Jeong [49] proved that the two-dimensional Euler equations
have a unique global solution with bounded vorticity for every bounded initial vorticity, without
decay at infinity, that is invariant under the rotation by 2π/m for some m ≥ 3, and in [50] they
proved the formation of a singularity in finite time for the three-dimensional Euler equations
                         BLOW-UP FOR THE NAVIER–STOKES EQUATIONS                                  5

on R3 in the class of the fields with octahedral symmetry, for data with bounded, piecewise
smooth vorticity. Problem 6 of the survey of Drivas and Elgindi [45] asks whether a scale-
invariant solution of the Euler equations in a symmetry class that is admissible in their sense
can develop a singularity in finite time. The rotating solutions of the surface quasi-geostrophic
equation of [25] (see Subsection 1.2) have a 3-fold symmetry.
1.2. Background. The self-similar solutions of Leray are the solutions of the first two equa-
tions of (1.2) that do not depend on s. Nečas, Růžička and Šverák [76] proved that the only
such solution with W ∈ L3 (R3 ) is zero, and Tsai [86, Theorems 1 and 2] proved the same for
W ∈ Lp (R3 ), 3 < p < ∞, and for the self-similar solutions that satisfy local energy estimates.
In particular a profile depends on s, since its slices lie in L6 (R3 ). For discretely self-similar
solutions, Open Problem 5.1 of [13] asks whether a backward solution with the Type I bound,
rotated or not, remains bounded up to time T ; by its self-similarity, a solution that remains
bounded is zero, and by Remark 1.2 a smooth solution that is not zero is the field (1.4) of a
profile. When R = I, Chae and Wolf [26, Theorems 1.1 and 1.3] proved that the Type I bound
holds for every smooth backward discretely self-similar solution that is continuous in time with
values in Lp (R3 ) for some p ∈ [3, ∞), and that a smooth solution with this bound is zero if
its scaling factor eS/2 is close enough to 1: the period S of a profile with R = I is bounded
below by a positive number that depends only on CW . Pineau and Vicol [79, Theorems 1.6
and 1.7] gave a quantitative proof and extended the statement to rotations R by an angle at
most cS, for a constant c > 0 that depends only on CW . Nonzero solutions with the Type I
bound that are self-similar up to a rotation at a constant angular speed in s would give profiles
for every period; the question of their existence goes back to Perelman, as recorded by Brad-
shaw and Tsai [13, Open Problem 5.2], and they are excluded for small and for large angular
speeds [79, Theorem 1.4]. Forward in time, self-similar and discretely self-similar solutions do
exist [64, 87, 12, 27], also up to a rotation [13].
   By the first bound of (1.3), u∗T (t) is bounded in weak L3 (R3 ), uniformly in t, and this gives
no bound in L3 (R3 ); for solutions that are bounded in L3 (R3 ), singularities are excluded by
the theorem of Escauriaza, Seregin and Šverák [52]. Singularities of Type I are excluded for
axisymmetric solutions [28, 29, 69, 81]. Albritton and Barker [1] proved that a suitable weak
solution with a Type I singular point exists if and only if there is a nontrivial mild bounded
ancient solution, in the sense of [69], with Type I decay. By [1, Remark 4.3], a nontrivial mild
ancient solution that is bounded in weak L3 (R3 ) along a sequence of times, as the field of a
profile is, gives a singular weak Leray–Hopf solution of the equations with a force. Theorem 1.1
assumes a discretely self-similar solution and concludes blow-up without force, from smooth,
compactly supported data on R3 and from smooth data on the torus.
   For other equations, blow-up near a self-similar profile has been proved for sets of data
of finite codimension: the linearization about the profile in self-similar variables has finitely
many unstable directions, which are controlled through the choice of the data. Merle, Raphaël,
Rodnianski and Szeftel [75] proved in this way the implosion of solutions of the compressible
Euler and Navier–Stokes equations from smooth spherically symmetric data, near the smooth
self-similar profiles of the Euler equations that they constructed in [74]. Buckmaster, Cao-
Labora and Gómez-Serrano [16] constructed smooth self-similar imploding solutions of the
isentropic compressible Euler equations in three dimensions for every adiabatic exponent larger
than 1 and proved, for the exponent 7/5, the implosion of solutions of the compressible Navier–
Stokes equations for a set of finite codimension of smooth radially symmetric data whose
density is bounded away from zero and constant at infinity. Cao-Labora, Gómez-Serrano, Shi
and Staffilani [24] removed the assumption of symmetry, for both systems, on R3 and on the
6                         LEVENT ALPÖGE AND TRISTAN BUCKMASTER

torus. Chen, Cialdea, Shkoller and Vicol [31] proved that the vorticity of smooth solutions
of the two-dimensional compressible Euler equations can blow up, through an axisymmetric
implosion that is stable in finite codimension in its radial part and for an open set of swirl
velocities; see [30] for every dimension d ≥ 3, and [36] for a family of smooth non-isentropic
implosions of the compressible Euler equations whose ground state is stable without symmetry
up to finitely many conditions on the data. Buckmaster and Iyer [19] constructed shocks for
the two-dimensional compressible Euler equations that converge in self-similar variables to an
unstable self-similar solution of the Burgers equation, and showed that this behavior is stable
modulo a two-dimensional subspace.
   For the incompressible Euler equations on R3 , Elgindi [47] constructed self-similar solutions
of class C 1,α , for some α > 0, that blow up in finite time, and Elgindi, Ghoul and Masmoudi [48,
Theorem 2] proved that this blow-up is stable in a class of axisymmetric perturbations, up to
one condition on the perturbation, which gives blow-up from data of finite energy. For the
nonlinear heat equation in one space dimension, Bricmont and Kupiainen [15, Theorems 1
and 2] constructed solutions that blow up with a prescribed profile by fixing finitely many
parameters of the data, one for each unstable direction of the profile as a fixed point of a
renormalization map, and for the Navier–Stokes equations with complex-valued solutions, Li
and Sinai [71] proved blow-up in finite time for suitable values of the parameters in ten-
parameter families of data. For the energy supercritical heat equation, Collot, Raphaël and
Szeftel [38, Theorem 1.2] proved that there is a Lipschitz manifold of finite codimension of
data without symmetry whose solutions blow up near a self-similar profile, up to scaling and
translation; these data are the profile, cut off at a large radius, plus a small perturbation.
   For wave equations, Donninger and Schörkhuber [43] proved the stability of a self-similar
blow-up by the Lyapunov–Perron method, following the work of Donninger on wave maps [42]:
the data are modified along the unstable direction, which there comes from the translations
in time, and the modification is undone at the end by a shift of the blow-up time. This is
the method that we use, except that we keep the modification of the data. Glogić, Hilditch
and Wallauch [57] constructed discretely self-similar blow-up solutions of a wave maps type
equation and proved their stability in finite codimension.
   Non-unique solutions of the Navier–Stokes equations have been produced by several meth-
ods. One is convex integration: Buckmaster and Vicol [20] proved that weak solutions with
finite kinetic energy are not unique; see also [18] and the survey [21]. Another is that of
Coiculescu and Palasek [37], who constructed, for the equations on the torus, data in BM O−1
with two distinct global solutions, both smooth at positive times, by a mechanism proposed
for a dyadic model of the equations. A third is based, like the proofs of blow-up recalled above,
on the linearization about a self-similar solution in self-similar variables. Jia and Šverák [65]
proved that Leray–Hopf solutions are not unique if the linearization about a forward self-similar
solution satisfies a spectral condition. After the work of Vishik on the two-dimensional Euler
equations [91, 92, 4], Albritton, Brué and Colombo [2] proved the non-uniqueness of Leray–
Hopf solutions with a force, and Buckmaster, Coiculescu and Li [17] proved it without force,
around a forward discretely self-similar solution whose linearization has a growing Floquet
mode. In these works an unstable direction of the linearized flow is followed forward in time,
and the solutions separate. Here we follow the stable directions toward the blow-up time, and
the solution converges to the profile.
   Several results in fluid mechanics have computer-assisted proofs, in which an analytic ar-
gument reduces the statement to finitely many conditions and a computation with rigorous
bounds for its errors verifies them; see the survey [58]. Chen and Hou proved in this way
the blow-up of the two-dimensional Boussinesq and the three-dimensional axisymmetric Euler
                          BLOW-UP FOR THE NAVIER–STOKES EQUATIONS                                   7

equations from smooth data in a domain with boundary [32, 33], following their proofs with
Huang of the finite-time blow-up, from smooth data, of the De Gregorio and Hou–Luo models
of the three-dimensional Euler equations [34, 35]; the smooth imploding profiles of [16] are
constructed with the help of a computer, and the global rotating solutions of the surface quasi-
geostrophic equation of Castro, Córdoba and Gómez-Serrano [25] rest on a computer-assisted
proof that the kernel of a linearized operator is one-dimensional. Time-periodic solutions of the
Navier–Stokes equations with a time-independent force have been constructed in this way on
the torus [90] and in a square [9], and so have discretely self-similar solutions of the Einstein–
scalar field system [80] and of the defocusing nonlinear wave equation [22]. Such proofs start
from approximate solutions computed numerically. Approximate self-similar solutions of the
Euler equations and of related models have also been found by neural networks [93, 94]. For
the Navier–Stokes equations, Guillod and Šverák [59] computed forward self-similar solutions
for axisymmetric scale-invariant data and found a symmetry-breaking bifurcation at large
data, and Ionescu, Jia and Palasek [63] constructed numerically unstable forward self-similar
solutions in the axisymmetric class without swirl; Hou [61] presented numerical evidence of
potentially singular behavior of axisymmetric solutions with smooth data of finite energy. We
compare the construction of Section 7 with those of [90, 9, 80, 22] in Subsection 7.4.
   Theorem 1.1 concerns the equations with zero force. With a force that is uniformly of class
C 1,α , the construction of Elgindi gives the blow-up of solutions of the Euler equations of finite
energy [47, Remark 1.4], and Córdoba and Martínez-Zoroa proved singularity formation for the
Euler equations with a force that is uniformly of class C 1,1/2−ε [39] and for the incompressible
porous media equation with a source that is smooth in space [40]. Singularity formation was
proved for the incompressible porous media, Boussinesq and Euler equations with forces that
are smooth in space and time up to the singular time in [7, 5, 6], and for the Euler equations
without force, from smooth periodic data, in [8].
1.3. Ideas of the proof. The field (1.4) of a profile is a smooth solution of (1.1), and we
construct the solutions of Theorem 1.1 as perturbations of it. We find the perturbation by a
contraction argument for the integral formulation of (1.1), which the field u∗1 satisfies on [0, 1)
(Proposition 4.1). We work in the space Ḣdiv  1 (R3 ) of the divergence-free fields in L6 (R3 ) with

square integrable gradient, with the norm kukḢ 1 = k∇ukL2 : by (1.3) the gradient of W (s, ·) is
square integrable, uniformly in s, whereas W (s, ·) itself need not be. On the torus we use the
corresponding space of periodic fields of mean zero (Subsections 2.1 and 3.6). By the classical
local theory of the equations in H 1 , a solution of the integral formulation in one of these spaces
whose datum is smooth and compactly supported, or smooth on the torus, is a smooth solution
(Lemma 2.3).
   We write u∗ = u∗1 . In the self-similar variables with blow-up time 1, in which u(t, x) =
(1 − t)−1/2 U (s, y), the equations (1.1) are the first two equations of (1.2) for U and a pressure.
The profile W is a solution of these that is periodic up to R, and we look for solutions U with
U (s) − W (s) → 0 as s → ∞.
                                                          √    √
   The norm of Ḣ 1 is subcritical: the scaling f 7→ µ f ( µ ·) of the equations multiplies it
by µ1/4 . In self-similar variables the linear flow without the terms that come from W is the
heat flow up to time 1 − e−s followed by this scaling with µ = e−s , and therefore its norm
on Ḣ 1 is at most e−s/4 . The flow linearized about W over one period, followed by the action
of R−1 on fields, is the monodromy operator M. It differs from the corresponding free flow
by a compact operator. Hence its essential spectral radius is at most e−S/4 < 1, and for a
suitable Θ ∈ (e−S/4 , 1) the spectrum of M outside the disc of radius Θ consists of finitely
many eigenvalues of finite multiplicity. For a self-similar solution forward in time this is the
8                          LEVENT ALPÖGE AND TRISTAN BUCKMASTER

argument of Jia and Šverák [65, Lemma 2.7]; for a solution that is periodic in self-similar time
it is used in [17, Section 19], with the same bound for the essential spectral radius.
   We assume nothing about these eigenvalues. In the program of [65] for non-uniqueness an
unstable eigenvalue is the hypothesis, and in [17] the main difficulty is to construct a solution
that has one. For blow-up it suffices that the eigenvalues outside the disc are finitely many: each
of them, whatever its modulus, is removed by a correction of the datum in a finite-dimensional
space. More precisely, let κ ∈ (0, 41 ) be defined by Θ = e−κS . We solve the equation linearized
about u∗ in the space of the perturbations v with
                                               1
                                  sup (1 − t) 4 −κ kv(t)kḢ 1 < ∞,
                                 0≤t<1

which are those of size e−κs in self-similar variables, and we leave the datum of v free in a finite-
dimensional space E, a complement of the invariant subspace of M on which its powers decay
faster than Θn . A Lyapunov–Perron argument over the periods of u∗ gives a bounded right
inverse of this linear problem (Section 4). Since the smooth, compactly supported, divergence-
                            1 (R3 ), we can choose E among them, and we need not know the
free fields are dense in Ḣdiv
regularity or the decay of the eigenfunctions of M. A contraction then gives, for every w0 in
             1 (R3 ) about u∗ (0) = W (0, ·), an element γ of E and a solution u on [0, 1) with
a ball of Ḣdiv
datum w0 + γ such that u − u∗ lies in this space. We modulate neither the blow-up time, nor
the location of the blow-up, nor the phase of u∗ in its period.
   For w0 we take the field u∗ (0), cut off far from the origin. The cutoff is applied to a vector
potential, so that the field  remains divergence-free: for 0 ≤ t < 1 and a radius r > 0 we let
ū(t) = curl η(·/r) A(t) , where A(t) is the vector potential of u∗ (t) given in (5.1) and η is a
                         

smooth cutoff that equals 1 on B1/2 and vanishes outside B1 . The field ū(t) equals u∗ (t) on
Br/2 and vanishes outside Br , and by (1.3) its distance from u∗ (t) in Ḣ 1 is at most a constant
multiple of r−1/2 , uniformly in t (Lemma 5.1). On R3 we take w0 = ū(0), for a large radius
r. Thus on R3 only the datum is cut off; the solution u∗ itself, which need not have finite
energy, is not. In [17, Section 19] the datum is truncated in the same way, through the same
potential. Jia and Šverák [65, Section 5], for the Navier–Stokes equations forward in time, and
Elgindi, Ghoul and Masmoudi [48, Subsection 2.6], for the Euler equations, pass likewise from
self-similar solutions of infinite energy to data of finite energy, by a cutoff at a large radius
which changes the datum little in the norm in which the solution is perturbed.
   On T3 we construct the solution for a small blow-up time T . By the scaling of the equations
this is the same as a large period at the blow-up time 1: if u solves the equations on [0, 1) ×
R3 /LZ3 , then L u(L2 t, Lx) solves them on [0, L−2 ) × T3 . We therefore keep the solution u∗
and the blow-up time 1, and we take the period L = T −1/2 large. The solution u∗ is not
periodic, so that on the torus there is no exact solution about which to linearize, and no
monodromy operator. We cut the fieldPu∗ off at the radius r = L/8 and extend it periodically:
the approximate solution is ua (t) = j∈Z3 ū(t, · − Lj), where ū(t) is the cutoff field above
for this radius, which equals u∗ (t) on BL/16 and vanishes outside BL/8 . On divergences the
heat flow composed with the Leray projection is the convolution with a kernel that decays like
|x|−4 , uniformly in time. The nonlinear term is a divergence, and so is the datum, since a
divergence-free field f is the divergence of x ⊗ f . Hence the flows of the datum and of the
nonlinear term on R3 differ from those of their periodic extensions on the torus, on a period
cell, by quantities of order L−1/2 and L−5/2 in Ḣ 1 (Lemma 6.1), and with the bound for the
cutoff the error by which ua fails to be a solution is of order L−1/2 (Lemma 6.2). For the
linearized problem we use that ū(t) vanishes outside BL/8 , so that in the equation linearized
about ua the term that comes from ua involves the perturbation only through its restriction
                          BLOW-UP FOR THE NAVIER–STOKES EQUATIONS                                    9

to this ball. We can therefore solve the linearized equation on R3 , with the right inverse of
the equation linearized about u∗ , and return to the torus; the map so obtained inverts the
equation linearized about ua up to an error of order L−1/2 . A Neumann series removes this
error when L is large. The same contraction as on R3 then gives a solution u on the torus of
period L, and uper (t, x) = L u(L2 t, Lx) is the solution of Theorem 1.1.
   Albritton, Brué and Colombo [3, Theorem 1.1] glued the construction of [2] into the torus,
where it gives non-unique Leray–Hopf solutions with a force, and Tao [84, proof of Theo-
rem 12.2] embeds a solution on R3 , cut off outside a ball, in a torus of large period, with a
force that is defined so that the cut-off field is a solution. Here the error of the cutoff is removed
by the contraction, and the equations on the torus hold without force.
   In the proof of Theorem 7.1 we correct an approximate profile to an exact one. Since
the period of a profile is not known in advance, we rescale the self-similar time, so that the
period becomes a coefficient of the equation. The equation linearized about a time-periodic
solution need not be invertible: it has, for instance, the derivative of the solution in time
in its kernel. We therefore add to the first equation of (1.2) a force term with finitely many
unknown coefficients and impose as many linear constraints on the solution. With the Fredholm
alternative, the a priori estimate of a certificate gives a bounded inverse of the linearized
problem with constraints. The approximate profiles depend on as many parameters as the force
term has coefficients, and the remaining conditions bound these coefficients so sharply that the
Schauder–Tychonoff fixed point theorem, applied to the parameters and to the quadratic term
together, gives a value of the parameters and a solution close to the approximate profile for
which the force term vanishes. We then show that this solution is smooth (Proposition 7.11),
and since its isometry is the identity, the theorem of Chae and Wolf recalled in Subsection 1.2
gives the Type I bound.
1.4. Organization and conventions. In Section 2 we reduce Theorem 1.1 to Propositions 2.1
and 2.2, on R3 and on the torus, and to Lemma 2.3 on smooth data. Section 3 contains the
properties of the heat flow and of the bilinear term that the rest of the paper uses; most of its
proofs, and that of Lemma 2.3, are in Appendix A. In Section 4 we construct the right inverse
of the equation linearized about u∗ and prove the lemma by which a contraction corrects an
approximate solution. In Section 5 we prove Proposition 2.1, and in Section 6 Proposition 2.2.
In Section 7, which can be read after Section 3, we define a certificate and prove Theorem 7.1. In
Appendix B we describe the form that the formalization prescribes for the data of a certificate,
and we derive the estimates that a certificate assumes for every perturbation from conditions
on finitely many functions and numbers, attached to data of this form. In Appendix C we
describe the numerical computation of the field from which the approximate profiles of the
certificate of Subsection 1.1 are formed, and of further fields of this certificate.
   From Section 2 on, the solutions that we construct blow up at time 1, except for the solution
on T3 , which is a dilation of one of them. The letters s and y are reserved for the self-
similar variables.  We write B̄r for the closure of the ball Br . For a field f on R3 we write
|∇f |2 = i |∂i f |2 , and similarly for derivatives of higher order. The Fourier transform is
           P

fˆ(ξ) = R3 e−2πix·ξ f (x) dx, with inverse F −1 , and the Fourier coefficients of a periodic function
        R

are fˆ(k) = [0,1]3 e−2πik·x f (x) dx, k ∈ Z3 . The Leray projection P multiplies fˆ(ξ), and on the
             R

torus fˆ(k), by the matrix Pξ = I − |ξ|−2 ξ ⊗ ξ, where P0 = I, so that on the torus it keeps the
mean.
1.5. Formalization. Theorems 1.1 and 7.1 have been formalized in the Lean 4 proof assis-
tant [41], using its mathematical library Mathlib [73], in the form in which an earlier version
of this paper stated them. The formal Theorem 1.1 has no rate: for every positive viscosity
10                        LEVENT ALPÖGE AND TRISTAN BUCKMASTER

it gives a smooth, compactly supported, divergence-free datum on R3 from which no smooth
solution of bounded energy exists for all time, and a smooth, periodic, divergence-free datum
from which no smooth solution with periodic velocity exists for all time. The formal Theo-
rem 7.1 obtains from a certificate a weak solution of the equation of a profile. For another
recent use of Lean in fluid mechanics, the verification of a certified computation of water waves,
see [82].
   The formalization also derives the conclusion of the formal Theorem 1.1, on R3 and on T3 ,
from conditions on finitely many functions and numbers, which do not include the estimates
that a certificate assumes for every perturbation. We describe these conditions in Appendix B.
The formalization contains no data: that the certificate of Subsection 1.1 satisfies them is
verified by a rigorous computation, involving interval arithmetic, which is not part of the
formalization.
1.6. AI statement. We used Anthropic models (Claude) throughout this work, and no others.
The work builds on unreleased work of the authors on computer-assisted proofs, in particu-
lar concerning the non-uniqueness of Leray–Hopf solutions in the style of Jia and Šverák [65]
and blow-up for fluid equations with boundary. It builds also on numerical work of the au-
thors on finding self-similar profiles, on our earlier papers on singularity formation, and on
our unreleased drafts on the forced hypodissipative Navier–Stokes equations and the forced
Navier–Stokes equations. The idea of pursuing discretely self-similar solutions came from the
paper of the second author with Coiculescu and Li on non-uniqueness [17]. The agents were
given a curated dossier of potential leads and then guidance after our strictly-uncontaminated
experiment producing unforced Euler and forced Navier–Stokes blow-up from our forced Euler
and forced hypodissipative Navier–Stokes manuscripts. A simpler mechanism sufficed to pro-
                                                                          501
duce unforced hyperdissipative Navier–Stokes blow-up, i.e. for ν · (−∆) 500 , and then a heavier
computation produced this profile. We used the enslopification skills of Scott Armstrong [10]
to create a draft from an initial Lean proof (Subsection 1.5); the draft followed that proof step
by step. We then rewrote it with Claude under our direction, citing standard results in place
of proofs and simplifying the argument over successive revisions, so that the proof given here
no longer follows the Lean proof.

3.5. Solutions in the sense of distributions. Furioli, Lemarié-Rieusset and Terraneo [55,
Proposition 1] proved that the Navier–Stokes equations in the sense of distributions, with a
pressure, are equivalent to their integral formulation for all fields that are square integrable
in time with values in the uniformly locally square integrable fields that vanish at infinity.
Dubois [46, Theorem 1] proved that, for such fields, both are equivalent to formulations without
a pressure, in which the equations are tested against divergence-free fields. The next lemma
follows from the theorem of Dubois.

                             4. The equation linearized about u∗
   In this section we construct a bounded right inverse of the equation linearized about the
field u∗ of a profile (Proposition 4.3), and we prove that a contraction corrects an approximate
solution once such a right inverse exists (Lemma 4.8).
   Throughout this section (W, q) is a profile with period S and isometry R, CW is the constant
of (1.3), λ = e−S , and u∗ = u∗1 is the field (1.4) with T = 1. In the proofs of this section the
constants in . depend only on the profile, unless we say otherwise. In particular they do not
depend on the time t or on the index n of a period.
4.1. The field u∗ . In the notation (3.4) the field (1.4) of the profile is u∗T (t) = D1/(T −t) W (s, ·)
with s = log(T /(T − t)). The field u∗ is a smooth function of (t, x) ∈ (−∞, 1) × R3 , divergence-
free in x, and by (1.3)

the last equation of (1.2); but a profile is not identically zero.                              
   In (4.2) the time 1 − λ(1 − t) is the time t advanced by the period S in the self-similar time
s = − log(1 − t). We write tn = 1 − λn for n ≥ 0, so that the intervals [tn , tn+1 ] are the periods
of u∗ . The next lemma expresses u∗ on each period through its restriction to the first, and it
gives the rate of u∗ .

Lemma 4.5. There exist a number Θ ∈ (e−S/4 , 1) and a bounded projection Pu of finite rank
on X that commutes with M, such that the restriction Mu of M to ran Pu is invertible and
                              Θ−n Mn (I − Pu ) +           Θn M−n

where the norms are operator norms on X.
Proof. We consider M and ρ−1      R Dλ e
                                         t1 ∆ on the complexification of X, where they have the

same norms and their difference is compact. By Lemma 4.4 the essential spectral radius of
M is that of ρ−1 R Dλ e
                        t1 ∆ , hence at most e−S/4 , and therefore every point of the spectrum of

M of modulus larger than e−S/4 is a pole of the resolvent of finite algebraic multiplicity [51,
Chapter IV, Paragraph 1.20 and (1.16)]. Poles are isolated, so that only finitely many points
of the spectrum lie in {|z| ≥ r} for each r > e−S/4 , and we can fix Θ ∈ (e−S/4 , 1) such that the
circle |z| = Θ does not meet the spectrum.
   Let P be the Riesz projection of M for the part of its spectrum in {|z| > Θ} [51, Chapter IV,
(1.6)–(1.8)]. It commutes with M, it has finite rank [51, Chapter IV, Paragraph 1.17], and it
maps X into X, because M is a real operator and I − P is the integral of (2πi)−1 (z − M)−1
over the circle |z| = Θ; let Pu be the restriction of P to X. The spectra of the restrictions of M
to ran(I − P ) and to ran P are the parts of the spectrum of M in {|z| < Θ} and in {|z| > Θ}.
Hence the first restriction has spectral radius less than Θ, and the second is invertible, with an
inverse of spectral radius less than Θ−1 ; in particular Mu is invertible. By the spectral radius
formula the two series of (4.11) converge.                                                       

                               7. From a certificate to a profile
   Theorem 1.1 assumes that a profile exists. In this section we define a certificate (Subsec-
tions 7.1 to 7.3) and prove that it gives a profile whose isometry is the identity (Theorem 7.1),
so that the conclusions of Theorem 1.1 hold on R3 and on the torus (Corollary 7.2). The proof
is the argument described at the end of Subsection 1.3, in which we correct an approximate
profile to an exact one.
   The constants of a certificate are explicit, so that a computation can verify the conditions
that are inequalities between numbers and norms of given fields [58, Section 1.1]. The esti-
mates (7.18), (7.19), (7.22), (7.32) and (7.33), which are to hold for every perturbation, are
assumed in this section; in Appendix B they are derived for data of a prescribed form.
30                          LEVENT ALPÖGE AND TRISTAN BUCKMASTER

7.1. The equation in rescaled time. Let S > 0. A field W of the self-similar variables
(s, y) has period S in s if and only if the field W(θ, y) = W (Sθ, y) has period 1 in θ, and in
the variables (θ, y) the first equation of (1.2) reads
(7.1)            TS W + (W · ∇)W + ∇q = 0,               TS = S1 ∂θ + 12 + 21 y · ∇ − ∆.
Throughout this section the time variable is θ = s/S. The fields of the variables (θ, y) that we
consider are defined on R × R3 and, with the exception of test fields, have period 1 in θ. We
write F (θ) = F (θ, ·), and we apply TS and the derivatives in y in the sense of distributions on
R × R3 . Let H be the Hilbert space of the measurable fields F : R × R3 → R3 of period 1 in θ
that are square integrable on (0, 1) × R3 , with
                                    Z 1Z
                                                                               1/2
                       hF, GiH =              F · G dy dθ,    kF kH = hF, F iH ;
                                     0   R3
we use the same notation for fields with values in the 3 × 3 matrices, with F · G = j,k Fjk Gjk .
                                                                                           P

Let Hdiv be the closed subspace of the divergence-free fields in H, and let P be the orthogonal
projection of H onto Hdiv . This is the Leray projection applied at each time: for almost every
θ it applies to F (θ) the multiplier Pξ of (2.2).
   The equation (7.1) is tested against divergence-free fields, so that it determines its terms
only up to gradients. For F and G in L1loc (R × R3 ; R3 ) we say that F and G are equal modulo
gradients, and write F ≃ G, if
          Z Z

the set of the fields v ∈ L2loc (R; X) of period 1 in θ such that
(7.4)                          ∆v ∈ H        and       TS v ∈ H.

The second condition says that S ∂θ v + 2 v + 2 (y · ∇)v is square integrable over a period. Only
this combination is: for v ∈ DS neither ∂θ v nor (y · ∇)v need belong to H. By Lemma 7.4
below, TS maps DS into Hdiv , and every v ∈ DS is, after a modification on a set of times of
measure zero, a continuous map from R to X; we always take this representative.
   A certificate may have symmetries. Let Γ be a set of pairs (R, θ0 ) of an isometry R ∈ O(3)
and a number θ0 ∈ R; it may be empty. A field F on R × R3 with values in R3 is Γ-invariant
if
(7.5)           F (θ + θ0 , y) = R F (θ, R−1 y)        for almost every (θ, y) ∈ R × R3

so that F is Γ-invariant if and only if ρR,θ0 F = F for every (R, θ0 ) ∈ Γ. The map ρR,θ0

and satisfies ρR,θ0 [(F · ∇)G] = (ρR,θ0 F · ∇)ρR,θ0 G. Hence the Γ-invariant fields form a closed

7.2. The approximate profiles and the problem for a perturbation. Let n ≥ 0 be an
integer, and let K be a compact convex subset of Rn , the set of the parameters; its shape
matters only in the last step of the proof, and it is specified in (7.29) below. For α ∈ K
we are given a period S(α) and a field Wαa : R × R3 → R3 , the approximate profile. The
function S is continuous on K, and 0 < S(α) ≤ Smax for a number Smax . The derivatives
∂θ Wαa and ∇Wαa exist, and Wαa (θ, y), ∂θ Wαa (θ, y) and ∇Wαa (θ, y) are continuous functions of
(α, θ, y) ∈ K ×R×R3 . For every α ∈ K the field Wαa has period 1 in θ and is divergence-free, its
second derivatives in y, in the sense of distributions on R × R3 , are locally integrable functions,
and
(7.7)       Wαa (θ + θ0 , y) = R Wαa (θ, R−1 y)        for all (θ, y) ∈ R × R3 and (R, θ0 ) ∈ Γ.
Moreover Wαa satisfies the bounds (1.3) of a profile, with a constant Ca in place of CW which
does not depend on α: for all (θ, y) ∈ R × R3 ,
(7.8)              |Wαa (θ, y)| ≤ Ca (1 + |y|)−1 ,          |∇Wαa (θ, y)| ≤ Ca (1 + |y|)−2 .
  The force that we add to the equation (7.1) is a combination of Γ-invariant fields b1 , . . . , bn ∈
H, the forcing fields, and b∗ ≥ 0 is a number such that

  A certificate consists of the set Γ and of the objects introduced in this subsection and in
Subsection 7.2, with the properties stated there, such that the following hold for every α ∈ K:
the a priori estimate (7.18) with (7.19) and (7.21); the lower bound (7.22); the bounds (7.23),
                          BLOW-UP FOR THE NAVIER–STOKES EQUATIONS                                    35

(7.24) and (7.28) for the residual and the approximate profile; the inequalities (7.25) to (7.27)
for ε and M ; and the conditions (7.32) to (7.37) on the parameter. The integers n and N may
be zero, and the set K may have empty interior. The definition does not prescribe the form in
which the fields of a certificate are given; the form that the formalization prescribes for them
is described in Appendix B.1.
7.4. The theorem and related constructions.
Theorem 7.1. Suppose that a certificate is given. Then there exist α ∈ K and a profile (W, q)
with period S = S(α) and isometry I, in the sense of (1.2) and (1.3), such that the field
v(θ, y) = W (Sθ, y) − Wαa (θ, y) belongs to DSΓ and satisfies
(7.38)        kvk2 + ω 2 |β a (α)|2 ≤ 4 ε2 ,
                  w                            sup kv(θ)k 1 ≤ M,
                                                            Ḣ     `(v) = ¯
                                                                          `(α).
                                                θ∈R
Moreover,
(7.39)    W (s + θ0 S, y) = R W (s, R−1 y)       for all (s, y) ∈ R × R3 and every (R, θ0 ) ∈ Γ.
Corollary 7.2. If there exists a certificate, then the conclusions of Theorem 1.1 hold, on R3
and on T3 , with the profile (W, q) of Theorem 7.1, the period S(α) and the isometry I.
Remark 7.3. For (R, θ0 ) ∈ Γ with θ0 > 0, the relation (7.39) is the last equation of (1.2)
with the period θ0 S and the isometry R, and neither the other equations of (1.2) nor the
bounds (1.3) involve the period or the isometry. Hence the profile (W, q) of Theorem 7.1 is
also a profile with period θ0 S and isometry R; in particular, when m = 2, with period S/2 and
isometry R for every R such that (R, 12 ) ∈ Γ.
   The computer-assisted constructions of time-periodic solutions of the Navier–Stokes equa-
tions with a time-independent force [90, Section 2], [9, Section 2] and of discretely self-similar
solutions [80], [22, Section 1.3.1] rescale the time, so that the period, or the frequency, becomes
a coefficient of the equation, and impose a condition that removes the translations in time;
see also the framework of [54, Section 3.3], in which the frequency is an unknown and a phase
condition is imposed. In these works the period is found together with the solution, by a
fixed point argument around an approximate solution computed numerically, and symmetries
reduce the problem, as the set Γ does here: a group of sixteen elements in [90], a change of
sign after half a period in [22]; for the certificate of Subsection 1.1, a rotation by π/19 about
an axis after half a period and a rotation by π about a perpendicular axis (Subsection B.1,
with κ = 19). Here the constraints play the role of the condition on the translations, and the
parameter, of which the period S(α) is a given function, is found together with the solution
by the Schauder–Tychonoff fixed point theorem (Proposition 7.10).
   A force in a fixed finite span, with as many linear constraints, is used in [89] for patterns of the
Swift–Hohenberg equation on a two-dimensional torus. There the force lies in the span of the
derivatives of an approximate solution in the directions of translation, and its coefficients are
shown to vanish by pairing the equation with the derivatives of the solution: by the invariance
of the energy under translations, this gives a linear system for the coefficients [89, Theorem 2.6],
whose matrix is then verified to be invertible. Here the pairing with the fields ψα,j only bounds
the coefficients of the force, as stated after (7.30). In the certificate of Subsection 1.1 only one of
the forcing fields accounts for the translations in time; the others were computed as directions
in which the inverse of the linearized operator is largest (Subsection C.4). In the construction
by Donninger and Schörkhuber [44, Section 2.3] of a self-similar blow-up solution of the cubic
Schrödinger equation, where there is one parameter, the parameter is found after the solution:
a term in the direction of a fixed function is added to a formal inverse of the linearized operator,
36                           LEVENT ALPÖGE AND TRISTAN BUCKMASTER

the modified equation is solved by a contraction for every small value of the parameter, and
the coefficient of the added term vanishes at some value, by the intermediate value theorem.
   Bounds for the inverse of a linearized operator, of which the a priori estimate (7.18) is an
instance, are obtained by computer for stationary problems in several ways: by a decomposition
into a coercive part and a compact part approximated by an operator of finite rank, as in [62,
Section 3] for the Navier–Stokes equations in forward self-similar variables on R3 , where the
strategy of Chen and Hou [32] is followed; by enclosures of eigenvalues, as in [95, Chapter 3]
for the stationary Navier–Stokes equations on an unbounded strip with an obstacle; and by an
approximate inverse built from Fourier series on a box [23].
   The rest of this section contains the proof of Theorem 7.1; a certificate is fixed throughout.
The constants of the certificate are written out in every estimate that leads to one of its
conditions. The other constants, denoted by C or implied by the symbol ., depend only on
Ca , unless we say otherwise, and they enter none of the conditions.
7.5. The free equation. In Eulerian variables the term (y · ∇)v of TS v is absent. For S > 0
and a locally integrable field v on R × R3 let
(7.40)               t = 1 − e−Sθ ,     x = e−Sθ/2 y,             u(t, x) = eSθ/2 v(θ, y).
These are the Eulerian variables of (1.4) with T = 1, for the self-similar time s = Sθ and with
v in place of W ; thus (t, x) ranges over (−∞, 1) × R3 , and u(t) = D1/(1−t) v(θ) with the scaling
Dµ of (3.4). By the chain rule,
(7.41)       ∂t u(t, x) − ∆u(t, x) = e3Sθ/2 TS v(θ, y),            ∆u(t, x) = e3Sθ/2 ∆v(θ, y)
in the sense of distributions on (−∞, 1) × R3 . Thus TS becomes the heat operator, and the
identity (7.42) becomes the energy identity for the heat equation.

on the subsequence, ∇vk → ∇v and ∆vk → ∆v weakly for the whole sequence, and by the
precompactness vk → v in L2 ((0, 1) × Br ) for every r > 0.                                     

                                         . kvk kL2 ((0,1)×B2r ) k∆vk kH + k∇vk kH ,
with a constant that depends only on φ, so that ∇vk → 0 in L2 ((0, 1) × Br ) as well. By (7.50)
the norm of Cα vk in L2 ((0, 1) × Br ) therefore tends to zero as k → ∞, for every r, and by (7.51)
its norm in L2 ((0, 1) × (R3 \ Br )) is at most supk k∇vk kH times a number that tends to zero
as r → ∞. Hence Cα vk → 0 in H.                                                                  

7.8. The nonlinear problem and the choice of the parameter. We look for a parameter
α ∈ K and a solution (v, β) of (7.12) at α with β a (α) + β = 0. The quadratic term is the
only nonlinear term of (7.12), and we take it as the unknown: for α ∈ K and a field g ∈ H we
consider the linearized problem
                                                 n
                                                                                            ¯
                                                 X
(7.57)                   TS(α) v + Cα v +              βi bi ≃ −Fαa − g,             `(v) = `(α),
                                                 i=1
                     Γ
for a pair (v, β) ∈ DS(α) ×Rn , so that such a pair solves (7.12) at α if and only if it solves (7.57)
with g = (v · ∇)v.
  The residual enters (7.57) through two consequences of the relation (7.10):
                                                               Z Z
(7.58)          PFαa ∈ Hdiv
                        Γ
                                      (α ∈ K),          α 7→             Fαa · ϕ dy dθ is continuous on K,
                                                               R    R3
the second for every divergence-free ϕ ∈ Cc∞ (R × R3 ; R3 ). To prove the first, let (R, θ0 ) ∈ Γ.
The left-hand side of (7.10) is invariant under ρR,θ0 , by (7.7), which holds at every point,
and because the fields bi are Γ-invariant. Since ρR,θ0 preserves the relation ≃, it follows that
ρR,θ0 Fαa ≃ Fαa . Both fields belong to H, so that ρR,θ0 PFαa = PρR,θ0 Fαa = PFαa . For the
continuity we replace Fαa by the left-hand side of (7.10) in the pairing with ϕ, and we integrate
by parts in the term that contains ∆Wαa . The pairing then involves only the functions Wαa ,
∂θ Wαa and ∇Wαa on the support of ϕ, the numbers 1/S(α) and βia (α), and the integrals of bi · ϕ.
These functions are continuous on the product of K with the support of ϕ, which is compact,
and S > 0 and β a are continuous on K. Hence the pairing is a continuous function of α.

   We find the parameter and the field g together, by the Schauder–Tychonoff fixed point
theorem for a map of the pairs (α, g): its second component is the quadratic term (v · ∇)v of
the solution (v, β) of (7.57), and its first is the point of K nearest to the vector α0 of (7.30)
formed with this β.

Proof. Let Z be the set of Γ-invariant fields g ∈ H with kgkH ≤ 2 cw ε M . Let α ∈ K, S = S(α)
and g ∈ Z. The field F = −Fαa − g belongs to H, and PF ∈ Hdiv       Γ , by (7.58) and because P

preserves Γ-invariance (Subsection 7.1). By Proposition 7.8 there is exactly one pair (v, β) in

   We first show that the solution at (α, g) satisfies the bounds (7.59) and that its quadratic
term (v · ∇)v belongs to Z. By (7.23) and (7.17),
                    kF kH ≤ δ(α) + 2 cw ε M,        [F ] ≤ δ0 (α) + 2 c0 cw ε M.

In particular kvkw ≤ 2ε and |β| ≤ 2ε/ω. Moreover kF kH ≤ δmax + 2 cw ε M , by (7.23), and

gives k(v · ∇)vkH ≤ 2 cw ε M , and (v · ∇)v is Γ-invariant, because v is. Thus (v · ∇)v ∈ Z.
   Let N be the nearest-point projection of Rn onto the closed convex set K, for the Euclidean
norm; it is continuous, and it is the identity on K [14, Theorem 5.2 and Proposition 5.3]. For
α ∈ K and g ∈ Z let
                                     G(α, g) = N (α0 ), (v · ∇)v ,
                                                                

where (v, β) is the solution at (α, g) and α0 is the vector (7.30) formed with this β. By what
we have proved, G maps K × Z into itself.
   We claim that G has a fixed point. The set Z is a closed ball of the closed subspace of Γ-
invariant fields in H. Hence it is convex and compact for the weak topology of H, and on Z this
topology is metrizable, because H is separable [14, Corollary 3.22 and Theorem 3.29]. Thus
K × Z is a compact convex subset of the locally convex space Rn × H, in which H carries its
weak topology, and by the Schauder–Tychonoff fixed point theorem [88, §2] every continuous
map of such a set into itself has a fixed point. Since the topology of K × Z is metrizable, and
since β a and N are continuous, it remains to prove the following. Let (αk , gk ) and (α, g) be
points of K × Z such that αk → α and gk → g weakly in H, and let (vk , βk ) and (v, β) be the
solutions at (αk , gk ) and at (α, g). Then βk → β in Rn and (vk · ∇)vk → (v · ∇)v weakly in H.
                             BLOW-UP FOR THE NAVIER–STOKES EQUATIONS                                    43

7.9. Proof of Theorem 7.1. Theorem 7.1 follows from Proposition 7.10 and from the next
proposition, which we prove in Subsection 7.10.
Proposition 7.11. Let S > 0, and let W : R×R3 → R3 be a field such that the map θ 7→ W(θ)
is continuous from R to X, has period 1 and is not identically zero. Suppose that every term
of TS W + (W · ∇)W is a locally integrable function on R × R3 , with TS as in (7.1), and that
                                       TS W + (W · ∇)W ≃ 0
in the sense of (7.2). Then there exist smooth functions W : R × R3 → R3 and q : R × R3 → R
such that (W, q) is a profile with period S and isometry I and
                            W (s, ·) = W Ss
                                              
(7.62)                                            in X       for every s ∈ R.
Proof of Theorem 7.1. Let α and (v, β) be as in Proposition 7.10, and let S = S(α) and
W = Wαa +v. We first show that W satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition 7.11. By Lemma 7.4,
v is a continuous map from R to X of period 1. So is θ 7→ Wαa (θ), by (7.8), the continuity
of ∇Wαa and dominated convergence. Hence θ 7→ W(θ) is a continuous map from R to X of
period 1.
   By (7.60), the relation displayed after (7.13) reads TS W + (W · ∇)W ≃ 0, that is, W
solves (7.1) modulo gradients. Every term of TS W + (W · ∇)W is a locally integrable function;
                                       BLOW-UP FOR THE NAVIER–STOKES EQUATIONS                                         45

for ∂θ v this follows from (7.4), since v and (y · ∇)v are locally square integrable. Finally, W is
not identically zero. Otherwise ∇Wαa = −∇v, and by the first bound of (7.59), since w ≥ w∗ ,
                                           w∗ k∇Wαa k2H = w∗ k∇vk2H ≤ kvk2w ≤ 4 ε2 ,
which contradicts (7.28).
   By Proposition 7.11 there is a profile (W, q) with period S and isometry I which satis-
fies (7.62), that is, W (Sθ, ·) = W(θ) in X for every θ ∈ R. Thus W (Sθ, ·) − Wαa (θ) = v(θ) in
X for every θ: the field v of the statement is the field v ∈ DSΓ of Proposition 7.10. The first
two relations of (7.38) are therefore the bounds (7.59) with β = −β a (α), and the third is the
second relation of (7.12).
   It remains to prove (7.39). Let (R, θ0 ) ∈ Γ. By (7.7), Wαa (θ + θ0 ) = ρR Wαa (θ) for every θ.
Since v is Γ-invariant, v(θ + θ0 ) = ρR v(θ) for almost every θ, by (7.5), and then for every θ,
because both sides are continuous with values in X. Hence W(θ + θ0 ) = ρR W(θ) for every θ,
that is, W (s + θ0 S, ·) = ρR W (s, ·) in X for every s ∈ R. Since W is continuous, the two sides
are equal at every point, which is (7.39).                                                      

   We now prove that u is smooth. Let a < b < 1. By (7.65), u is a bounded measurable
function on [a, b] × R3 , and k∇Oτ kL1 . τ −1/2 , by (3.9). Hence, for every t ∈ (a, b], the integral
in (7.64) converges absolutely at every point of R3 , and by Fubini’s theorem (7.64) holds almost

mild solution of the Cauchy problem for (1.1) with the bounded datum u(a), in the sense of [69,
Section 4], and by [69, Proposition 4.1], for all integers k, l ≥ 0 the function (t − a)k/2+l ∇kx ∂tl u
is bounded on (a, b) × R3 , where the derivatives are taken in the sense of distributions. Since
a < b < 1 are arbitrary, u is equal almost everywhere on (−∞, 1) × R3 to a smooth function,
all derivatives of which are bounded on [a, b] × R3 whenever a < b < 1. For every t < 1 the
value of this function at time t is the element u(t) of X: this holds for almost every t, and
both depend continuously on t. From now on u denotes this smooth function.
   We turn to the pressure. Let G = ∂tRu − ∆u + (u · ∇)u, a smooth field on (−∞, 1) × R3 . Since
u is smooth, the identity (3.14) gives R3 G(t) · ψ dx = 0 for every t < 1 and every ψ ∈ Cc,div   ∞ ,

so that curl G(t) = 0 and G = −∇p with

                                                      0
a smooth function on (−∞, 1) × R3 . Thus the smooth functions u and p satisfy (1.1) on
(−∞, 1) × R3 .
   Since W has period 1, the second identity of (3.5) gives u(1 − e−S (1 − t)) = DeS u(t) for
t < 1, which is the relation (1.5) with T = 1, R = I and λ = e−S . Both sides are continuous
functions on R3 , and with 1 + eS t in place of t we obtain
                     u(1 + t, x) = eS/2 u 1 + eS t, eS/2 x                 (t < 0, x ∈ R3 ).

Thus the function (x, t) 7→ u(1 + t, x) on R3 × (−∞, 0) is discretely self-similar with the factor
eS/2 > 1, in the sense of [26, (1.2)]. Moreover it is smooth, it is continuous in t with values
in L6 (R3 ), by (3.1), and it solves (1.1) with the pressure (x, t) 7→ p(1 + t, x). These are the
hypotheses of [26, Theorem 1.1], with the exponent 6, and the theorem gives a constant C
such that

  It remains to return to the self-similar variables. For (s, y) ∈ R × R3 let

These functions are smooth, and W (s, ·) = De−s u(1 − e−s ) = W(s/S) for every s, which
is (7.62). In particular W is not identically zero, and W (s + S, ·) = W (s, ·) in X for every
s, hence at every point, because W is continuous; this is the third equation of (1.2) with
R = I. The variables (s, y) and (t, x) are related as in (7.40) with S = 1, and by the chain
rule (7.41) the equations (1.1) for (u, p) are the first two equations of (1.2) for (W, q). Finally,
with t = 1 − e−s and x = e−s/2 y the bound (7.66) reads |W (s, y)| ≤ C (1 + |y|)−1 for all
(s, y) ∈ R × R3 , which is the first bound of (1.3). By Remark 1.2, the second bound follows
from the first and from the first two equations of (1.2), with a constant that depends only on
C. Thus (W, q) is a profile with period S and isometry I.                                         

is continuous in t0 ∈ [a, t), with norm at most CK (t − t0 )−3/4 max[a,b] kukX max[a,b] kvkX , and
it is integrable. After the substitution τ = t − t0 the continuity of the integral in t follows by
dominated convergence.                                                                           

A.2. Proof of Lemma 2.3. We deduce the lemma from the local theory in H 1 , in the form
given by Tao [84, Section 5], which concerns solutions in a smaller class. An H 1 mild solution
on [0, b] in the sense of [84, p. 31], without force and with a divergence-free datum u0 ∈ H 1 (R3 ),
is a divergence-free field ũ ∈ L∞ ([0, b]; H 1 (R3 )) ∩ L2 ([0, b]; H 2 (R3 )) which satisfies the integral
equation [84, (11)] with the datum u0 ; its pressure p̃ is determined by ũ(t) at each time t,
through the formula [84, (9)]. The periodic H 1 mild solutions of [84, p. 29] are defined in the
same way on the torus. We show that such a solution with the datum u(0) coincides with u as
long as it exists, and that its norm in H 1 remains bounded. By the alternative of [84] between
existence and blow-up in H 1 , it then exists on [0, b] for every b < T , and u has the smoothness
that [84] proves in this class.
                           BLOW-UP FOR THE NAVIER–STOKES EQUATIONS                                     49

Proof of Lemma 2.3. Let u be as in the first assertion, let u0 = u(0) and 0 < b < T , and let ũ be
an H 1 mild solution on an interval [0, b0 ], 0 < b0 ≤ b, with datum u0 . By [84, Theorem 5.4(i)]
it is a continuous map from [0, b0 ] to H 1 (R3 ), and since its values are divergence-free and
H 1 (R3 ) ⊂ L6 (R3 ), it is a continuous map from [0, b0 ] to X. Since div ũ = 0, the integrand
of [84, (11)] is K(t − t0 )(ũ(t0 ), ũ(t0 )), so that the integral converges in X as well, to the same
field, and is continuous in t with values in X, by Lemma 3.3. Both sides of the equation are
therefore continuous in t with values in X, so that it holds at every time. Thus ũ is a mild
solution on [0, b0 ], and ũ = u on [0, b0 ], by Lemma 3.5. Moreover, u0 is a Schwartz field. Hence
ũ and p̃ are smooth on [0, b0 ]×R3 , and all their derivatives in t and x are bounded on [0, b0 ] with
values in L2 (R3 ) [84, Theorem 5.4(iv)]. The formula [84, (10)] is the integral form of (1.1), so
that (ũ, p̃) is a smooth solution of (1.1) on [0, b0 ] × R3 with datum u0 . By these bounds we may
                                                                        d
multiply (1.1) by ũ and integrate by parts over R3 , which gives dt      kũ(t)k2L2 = −2 k∇ũ(t)k2L2 ≤ 0.
Hence kũ(t)kL2 ≤ ku0 kL2 and, since ũ = u on [0, b0 ],
                         kũ(t)kH 1 ≤ ku0 kL2 + max kukḢ 1        (0 ≤ t ≤ b0 ).
                                                  [0,b]

   By [84, Corollary 5.8], either there is an H 1 mild solution on [0, b] with datum u0 , or there
are T∗ ∈ (0, b] and a map ũ from [0, T∗ ) to H 1 (R3 ) with kũ(t)kH 1 → ∞ as t → T∗ , whose
restriction to [0, b0 ] is an H 1 mild solution with datum u0 for every b0 < T∗ . The second case
does not occur, because the bound above holds on [0, b0 ] for every b0 < T∗ . Hence there is an
H 1 mild solution on [0, b] with datum u0 . As we have shown, it is the restriction of u to [0, b],
it is a smooth solution of (1.1) on [0, b] × R3 , and its norm in L2 does not exceed that of u0 .
Since b < T is arbitrary, this proves the first assertion.
   Now let u be as in the second assertion, with the smooth datum u0 , and let 0 < b < T .
The argument is the same, with the periodic H 1 mild solutions, whose velocity and pressure
are periodic, with KT in place of K, and with [84, Theorem 5.1(i) and (iv) and Corollary 5.2]
in place of [84, Theorem 5.4(i) and (iv) and Corollary 5.8]. The datum has mean zero, as the
theory on the torus requires [84, Theorem 5.1]. A periodic H 1 mild solution with this datum
is a continuous map into H 1 (T3 ) [84, Theorem 5.1(i)] whose values are divergence-free and
have mean zero [84, (39)], and hence a continuous map into Ḣdiv  1 (T3 ), and Lemmas 3.3 and 3.5

apply in this space by Lemma 3.7. The second case of [84, Corollary 5.2] does not occur: in
that case ũ = u on [0, T∗ ), and ku(t)kH 1 (T3 ) . max[0,b] kukḢ 1 (T3 ) for 0 ≤ t < T∗ , by (3.16).
Hence the restriction of u to [0, b] is a smooth solution of (1.1) on [0, b] × T3 , and since b < T
is arbitrary, this proves the second assertion.                                                     

                      Appendix B. Certificates in the formalization
   In this appendix we describe the form that the formalization (Subsection 1.5) prescribes for
the data of a certificate, and we derive, from conditions on finitely many functions and numbers
attached to data of this form, the estimates (7.18), (7.19) and (7.22) and the bounds (7.32)
to (7.34). The result is Proposition B.14: data of this form that satisfy these conditions
are a certificate in the sense of Section 7. We prove it here except for two statements, whose
proofs are in the formalization and which we only describe: Proposition B.1, the inequality for a
multiplier with which the a priori estimate (7.18) begins (Subsection B.2), and Proposition B.4,
the inequalities for the cells of a mesh of the meridian plane, from which the rest of this estimate
is assembled along a tree of matrices (Subsection B.3). In the formalization the two are stated
for larger classes of perturbations and of right-hand sides. By the conditions of this appendix
we mean those of Subsections B.1 to B.4 together with the further ones that are named after
50                              LEVENT ALPÖGE AND TRISTAN BUCKMASTER

Proposition B.14; together they are the hypothesis of the formal statement. The radial phase
of Subsection B.1 and the weights of Subsection B.2 are explained in Appendix C.
B.1. The form of the data of a certificate. In the formalization the fields of a certificate are
of two kinds. The approximate profiles, the reference profile, the forcing fields and the adjoint
fields are finite sums of harmonics in θ and in the angle about the y3 axis, with coefficients
formed from piecewise polynomials and from a real function of the radius, the radial phase. The
fields Ξi of the constraints and the fields ek are given by polynomials in cylindrical coordinates
on finitely many cells of the meridian plane. The weight of (7.14) is constant: w ≡ w∗ = 39       50 .

and ez = (0, 0, 1). For an angle φ0 we write Rφ0 for the rotation by φ0 about the y3 axis, and
we write R∗ for the rotation by π about the y1 axis, R∗ y = (y1 , −y2 , −y3 ). Let κ ≥ 3 be an
integer. The set Γ contains the two pairs

Across the lines of the partitions they are joined to a prescribed order: the derivatives in σ up
to the order 2 and in η up to the order 1 are continuous for A, up to the orders 1 and 2 for B, 2
and 2 for S, and 1 and 1 for T, so that every component in (B.7) and (B.8) is continuously dif-
ferentiable, and S and T vanish at σ = 0. The field (B.6) is then of class C 1 on R × R3 and has
period 1 in θ; its second derivatives are bounded functions, which are the classical derivatives
away from the spheres and the cones about the y3 axis that correspond to the points of the
partitions and away from the sphere σ = σg . The set A contains (−ι, −l) with (ι, l), with the
complex conjugate data, so that the terms of (B.6) come in conjugate pairs; and it contains
(ι[ , l) with (ι, l), with A(σ, −η) and −B(σ, −η) in place of A(σ, η) and B(σ, η), while S and T
are odd in η. With these relations the field is invariant under the second pair of (B.1), and it
is invariant under the first because its harmonics are those of (B.2). It is therefore Γ-invariant,
since every element of Γ is composed of powers of these two pairs and of a translation of θ by
an integer.
    The behaviour at the axis, at the origin and at infinity is prescribed by explicit factors. For
ι1 6= 0 the factor (1 − η 2 )2 = (r/|y|)4 makes each component vanish on the axis, and σ h0 with
h0 ≥ 2 makes it vanish to second order at the origin. For ι1 = 0 the vectors p and a of (B.4)
vanish on the axis, and at the origin only the term with γ contributes to the gradient, by the
linear field −(γ/L) Eι,l (θ, 0) (y1 , y2 , −2y3 ). The factor (1 − σ)h = (L/(|y| + L))h gives the decay:
with the conditions on f , if h ≥ h∗ in every term, then the field (B.6) and its derivative in
θ are bounded by a constant multiple of (1 + |y|)−h∗ , and its first and second derivatives in
y by constant multiples of (1 + |y|)−h∗ −1 and (1 + |y|)−h∗ −2 . For h∗ = 1 these contain the
bounds (7.8).

   The reference profile does not depend on α: Wαr = W r for every α ∈ K, where W r is one
field of the form (B.6), with a length, a radial phase and data of its own and with the integer κ
of (B.1). Its piecewise polynomials are given in a basis of Hermite type: in each variable and
on each interval of the partition, by their derivatives up to an order D − 1 at the two ends and
by finitely many further coefficients, where the integer D exceeds by at least one the order to
which they are joined. The forcing fields are also of the first kind: bi is a field of the form (B.6),
with a length Li and a radial phase of its own, with γ = 0 and with piecewise polynomials that
vanish for σ ≥ σi , for a point σi < 1 of their partitions. Thus bi is of class C 1 , divergence-free
and Γ-invariant, and it vanishes for |y| ≥ Li σi /(1 − σi ). The adjoint fields do not depend on
α either: for j ∈ J and every α ∈ K the field ψα,j is one field ψj of the form (B.6), with one
length, one radial phase and one function g for all j and with h ≥ 3 in every term. Nor do
the rows of the matrix Y (α) with index in J , which alone enter the conditions of a certificate,
depend on α; we write Yjl for their entries.

B.2. The multiplier inequality. The a priori estimate (7.18) is obtained with a multiplier,
which acts on the modes of a perturbation: the pairing of the linearized equation with the
multiplier is bounded from below by the integral of a quadratic form of the modes (Proposi-
tion B.1), and a lower bound for this integral then gives (7.18) (Lemma B.2). For a field v and

   The estimate has weights. The harmonics (B.2) are divided into finitely many classes, and
to the class of ι belong two functions Dι and Zι on R3 , which are also those of the class of
−ι. There are two finite sets of indices, Pλ and Pµ . To an index p of either set belong a set
Np of harmonics, disjoint from −Np , a rate of rotation νp (α) ∈ R, a weight, which we write
λp ≥ 0 if p ∈ Pλ and µp ≥ 0 if p ∈ Pµ , a function which is to bound |W r + 21 y − νp ez × y|
and which we call the transport bound of p, finitely many positive auxiliary functions and, if
p ∈ Pµ , a positive number; and there is a constant cµ ∈ (0, 1). All these functions are invariant
under the rotations about the y3 axis and are given in the variables (B.3), with a length of
their own and separately for y3 ≥ 0 and y3 < 0: Dι and Zι are piecewise polynomials in (σ, |η|)
of class C 2 , constant near the origin, with Dι ≥ 0; λp is 1 − σ times a continuous piecewise
polynomial which is constant at σ = 0, and µp is (1 − σ)2 times one; the auxiliary functions
are integer powers of 1 − σ times functions that are constant on the rectangles of a partition.
Of all this only the rates νp (α) depend on α. No set Np contains a harmonic ι with |ι2 | ≤ j∗

for the quadratic form of S, s2 for the operator norm of the mean V̄ of V over time and angle,
taken in the frame (er , eφ , ez ), and s3 for the operator norm of V − V̄ in this frame. Thus s2
and s3 are nonnegative, and so is s1 , because the trace of S vanishes.
   Each cell Q has three finite sets of harmonics, each of which contains −ι with ι: the harmonics
that are resolved in Q; among them the harmonics of the triples ϕ of Subsection B.1 on Q; and
a set of harmonics with ι1 6= 0, disjoint from the resolved ones, which we call eliminated. On
a cell that meets the axis only harmonics with ι1 = 0 are resolved. Each resolved harmonic of
a cell has two degrees d0 ≥ 1 and d ≥ d0 + 2, with d0 ≥ 2 if it is the harmonic of a triple ϕ
on Q, and the polynomials ϕr , ϕφ , ϕz of such a triple have degrees at most (d0 , d0 − 1), (d0 , d0 )
and (d0 − 1, d0 ) in (r, z).
   Each cell has polynomial models of W r , of curl W r , of S and of V, given as Ξi is in (B.10),
with numbers that bound their errors, and there is a number approximating 1/S(α), with a
bound for its distance from 1/S(α) on K.
   For each resolved harmonic the cell has finitely many unknowns, linear functionals of one
mode of a perturbation and of its pressure in the cell: the coefficients of its gradient and of
its components in products of orthonormal polynomials of degree at most d and d − 1 in each
variable, moments of its traces and of the pressure on the sides, and values at corners. To
these are added β ∈ Rn , a vector ζ ∈ Rn and finitely many further unknowns. The unknowns
of all the cells are expressed through two finite sets of real unknowns, the global unknowns, by
a matrix XQ for each cell. Those of the first set are determined by a perturbation and include
β; those of the second set, which we call free, include the ζi . We write xg = (xp , xf ) for a real
vector of global unknowns, with xp in the first set and xf in the second. The matrices XQ are
formed by relations that hold for every perturbation, such as the continuity of a trace across
a side, the symmetry at the plane z = 0, or the divergence equation on a side. The vectors of
unknowns of a cell are complex, and hAx, xi denotes the real part of the form of a matrix A
on a vector x.
58                           LEVENT ALPÖGE AND TRISTAN BUCKMASTER

   A cell has a matrix AQ on its unknowns and, for each resolved harmonic, two numbers I2 > 0
and I02 ≥ 0 and a bounded measurable field AQ,ι (r, z) of Hermitian 12 × 12 matrices, which act
on the pairs (G, v). On the cell, I2,ι ≥ I2 and I02,ι ≥ I02 . The form of AQ,ι (r, z) on a pair (G, v) is
at most the sum of the other three terms of Iι minus a form with constant coefficients, which
bounds the errors of the polynomial models; and, up to forms whose coefficients are constants
or constants divided by r, it is at least L(G, v). The matrix AQ enters through one comparison
with explicit forms: for every vector x = XQ xg ,
(B.26)             h(AQ − EQ ) x, xi ≤ h(PQ + BQ − TQ ) x, xi + bQ (x) + eQ (x).
Here EQ is formed from I2 , I02 and the integrals of AQ,ι against the polynomials of the cell;
PQ is at most the form that J induces on the polynomials of the resolved modes through the
models of S and of V, minus terms for the harmonics that are not resolved; BQ is at most the
pairing of the gradients of the resolved modes with the harmonics of ∇bi , with the weights βi ,
and of Ξi , with the weights 2ζi ; TQ is an explicit form on the coefficients of highest degree of
the gradient; and the forms bQ and eQ come from the eliminated harmonics, which have no
unknowns, and from the pairings of the right-hand side with the fields of the triples ϕ. The
component of a resolved mode that is orthogonal to the polynomials of degree at most d is
controlled by I2 : the terms that it has to absorb are at most

   The proof of Proposition B.4 is in the formalization (Subsection 1.5); we describe what it
consists of. The finiteness of I − follows from lower bounds for the coefficients of Iι , which are
among the conditions, and, for J, from the bounds for the derivatives of W r , with Hardy’s
inequality. For (B.32), the functions v and ∇v are expanded at each point (r, z) in the time and
the angle; their coefficients are the modes, and by Parseval’s identity the mean of a product is
a sum over the harmonics. The first term of (B.18) is at least −s1 | curl v|2 . The other two are
60                          LEVENT ALPÖGE AND TRISTAN BUCKMASTER

a bilinear formpin v and ∇v which, with a matrix of trace zero in the place of V, is in absolute
value at most 7/2 times the operator norm of the matrix times |v| |∇v|; this is applied to V̄
mode by mode and to V − V̄ at each time and angle, with Young’s inequality.
   In a cell, the vector XQ xg consists, up to a vector on which the form of AQ does not depend,
of the values of the unknowns for the modes of v and for the pressure that the equation
determines, with the free unknowns of xf . A resolved mode is the sum of the polynomial
whose coefficients are unknowns and of a remainder orthogonal to the polynomials. By the
first bound for AQ,ι , the integral of the term Iι of a resolved harmonic is at least that of the
form of AQ,ι on the mode plus I2 and I02 times the parts of the integrals of |∇2 v̂ι |2 and |∇ curl v̂ι |2
that the unknowns do not determine. In the expansion of J¯ a harmonic of S or of V couples two
modes whose harmonics differ by it. The couplings between the polynomials of resolved modes
are computed with the polynomial models. An eliminated mode is removed pointwise, through
its angular derivative, which requires ι1 6= 0. All other couplings are bounded, as in (B.32), by
the expressions (B.25) for the remainders and for the modes that are not resolved, which the
second bound for AQ,ι and the differences Iι − Iι◦ absorb. The pairings of F with the fields of
the triples ϕ are computed from the equation by an integration by parts over the cell; rQ,1 is
                                                         3
a form of these pairings, whose sum over the cells is 50   [F ]2 /4π, and rQ,2 is the pairing over Q
of the gradients of the modes with the harmonics of ∇bi and of Ξi , up to errors bounded by
rQ,3 |β|2 , by nb |β|2 and by a part of rQ,0 . The remainders are absorbed by I2 and I02 times the
parts of the two integrals that the unknowns do not determine, by means of (B.27), and by TQ ,
and the comparison (B.26) then gives (B.33). The other part of rQ,0 belongs to the rectangles:
the mode v̂ι[ is divergence-free, and its component along ez on the plane z = 0 is minus that
of v̂ι , by (B.24), so that an integration by parts over Π and the conditions on the rectangle
bound the form of 2 CΠ on the moments of this trace by the integral over Π of |v̂ι[ |2 + |v̂−ι[ |2
times a function which is part of the function a0 of these harmonics. The inequality (B.34)
follows by adding over the cells.
   The last assertion is obtained, for each component of a mode with ι1 = 0, by completing a
square with the function r/|y|2 , for the components along er and eφ , which vanish on the axis,
or z/|y|2 , for the component along ez , which vanishes on the plane z = 0 by (B.24), and by
integrating over the region.

Proposition B.8. Suppose that the data satisfy the conditions of this appendix. Then, for
every α ∈ K and all v, β and F as in (7.18), we have I − < ∞, and (B.21) holds.
Proof. By Proposition B.4, I − < ∞. If I + = ∞, the left-hand side of (B.21) is +∞, and

for the regions {r > a or z > a}, the integral of Iι◦ over each region given for ι is nonnegative,
and so are its integral over the complement, in which these regions lie and which they cover
