of all vectors of global unknowns. Returning to v, β and F , we write (xp , 0) as the sum of an
element of Y ⊥ and of a vector (0, −xf ). Then (xp , xf ) ∈ Y ⊥ , and hN xg , xg i ≥ 0 for this xf . 

   By Theorem 7.1 and Corollary 7.2, data as in Proposition B.14 therefore give a profile, and
the conclusions of Theorem 1.1 on R3 and on T3 . The certificate that will be released consists
of data of the form of Subsection B.1 that satisfy the conditions of this appendix.
   The conditions that we have not written out are of the same kinds as those above. Besides
the expressions of the coefficients of (B.16) and lower bounds for them, they concern the
normalization of the pressure in the cells, under which the matrix of a cell is invariant when a
constant is added to its pressure; the rules by which the unknowns of the cells are expressed
through the global ones; the parts of W r and of its derivatives that the polynomial models
leave out, and the groups of harmonics that they couple; the bounds for the errors that are
absorbed in each cell; the rectangles near the plane y3 = 0; and the symmetry of these data
under ι 7→ −ι. Apart from inequalities between finitely many numbers, the conditions comprise
identities between polynomials, bounds for given functions at every point of a cell or of a region,
uniformly in time and angle, bounds for integrals over a period and R3 , and comparisons of
matrices.

              Appendix C. The computation of the approximate profile
   In this appendix we describe how the field from which the approximate profiles of the
certificate of Subsection 1.1 are formed was found, and how further fields of this certificate
were computed from it, and we show this field (Figures 1 to 4).
C.1. The approximate profile. We write W̄ for the computed approximate profile, as a
field of the self-similar variables (s, y), and S̄ for its period in s. In the variable θ = s/S̄ it is
a field of the form (B.6) of Subsection B.1, and the approximate profiles of the certificate are
formed from it: in a ball about the origin they are the affine combinations of W̄ with the fields
of Subsection C.4, as in (B.9).
   For this certificate the integer κ of (B.1) is 19. The field W̄ is a sum of harmonics (B.2)
with this integer, and its terms satisfy the relations of Subsection B.1. Hence W̄ is invariant
under the rotation by 2π/19 about the y3 axis and under the rotation R∗ by π about the y1
axis, and an advance of s by half the period rotates it by the angle π/19:
                                                  −1
                   W̄ s + 12 S̄, y = Rπ/19 W̄ s, Rπ/19          (s ∈ R, y ∈ R3 ).
                                                          
                                                       y
The harmonics Eι with ι2 = 0 depend on θ and φ only through φ − 2πθ/19, and those with
ι1 = ι2 only through φ + 2πθ/19. Thus W̄ contains two patterns that turn about the y3 axis
in opposite senses, each by the angle 2π/19 in one period; the rotation R∗ exchanges them,
                         BLOW-UP FOR THE NAVIER–STOKES EQUATIONS                               71




       Figure 2. The computed approximate profile W̄ at s = 0 in the meridional
       half plane, in the coordinates r = (y12 + y22 )1/2 and z = y3 ; angle brackets denote
       the mean over the angle φ. Left: the azimuthal vorticity of the mean of W̄ ,
       with level lines of its stream function. Middle: the azimuthal component of the
       mean. Right: the modulus of the harmonic e19iφ of W̄ , on a logarithmic scale
       over four decades.

and every other harmonic is the product of a harmonic of the first pattern and of one of the
second. The computed period is S̄ ≈ 0.36.
   Figures 1, 2 and 3 show W̄ at s = 0. Its vorticity is concentrated in a column about the y3
axis and, on either side of the plane y3 = 0, in a layer that leaves the column and is divided
into nineteen spiral arms (Figure 1). The mean of W̄ over the angle φ is an axisymmetric field
with swirl. Its azimuthal component has opposite signs on the two sides of the plane y3 = 0, as
the invariance under R∗ requires. Its azimuthal vorticity is concentrated in a thin layer, which
runs along the plane at a distance from it and then turns into the direction of the axis, where
it encloses the column, and the level lines of its stream function follow this layer (Figure 2,
left and middle). The part of W̄ that depends on φ is of the same order of magnitude as the
mean. Its first harmonics, e±19iφ , are largest near the plane, about the outer end of the layer
(Figure 2, right), and in a horizontal plane through the layer the vertical component of the
vorticity forms nineteen spiral arms, which end in nineteen vortices (Figure 3). The mean over
φ has period S̄/2 in s, by the symmetry.
   By the symmetry the norm k∇W̄ (s)kL2 has period S̄/2, and in the computation it varies
over a period by less than a thousandth of its value. Figure 4 shows how W̄ is distributed
in |y|. At the origin W̄ vanishes, and its gradient there is a multiple, which depends on s, of
the gradient of the field (y1 , y2 , −2y3 ) (Subsection B.1). The weighted suprema of W̄ and of
its first and second derivatives that are shown there are largest for |y| between 10 and 100,
and for larger |y| they are nearly constant. The bounds (7.8) hold for W̄ by its form; in the
computation the constant that they require is of order 105 .
   Thus the computed field decays like |y|−1 , and the radial phase of Subsection B.1 is made
for such a far field. In W̄ the radial phase is

frequency of Eι in θ. The functions (B.5) are then
                                              2πil h            |y|2     i
                Eι,l (θ, y) = e19iι1 φ exp              log 1 +          − s   ,   s = S̄θ,

                         BLOW-UP FOR THE NAVIER–STOKES EQUATIONS                                  73

and for large |y| the bracket is 2 log |y| − s up to a constant and a term of order |y|−2 . In the
Eulerian variables of (1.4) we have 2 log |y|−s = 2 log |x|−log T . Hence a field of the form (B.6)
with these functions Eι,l , whose coefficients are asymptotic, for large |y|, to functions of the
angles divided by |y|, corresponds through (1.4) to a field that has a limit as t → T at every
x 6= 0. Without the radial phase the coefficients of such a field would oscillate in log |y|, with
frequencies proportional to l, in every ring of the partition out to infinity.
C.2. The discretization and Newton’s method. The computation uses the first two equa-
tions of (1.2) in the form
(C.1)    ∂s W + 12 W + 12 (y · ∇)W + curl W × W + curl curl W + ∇H = 0,             div W = 0,
where H = q + 12 |W |2 . The unknown field is of the form (B.6), with κ = 19, with the radial
phase above and with the exponents h0 = 2 and h = 1. Thus the unknowns are the coefficients
of the piecewise polynomials A, B, S and T of (B.7) and (B.8) and the numbers γ, for finitely
many harmonics, and the period. All the harmonics share one partition of the variables (σ, η)
of (B.3), into rings in the radius, the last of which reaches infinity, and into intervals of η ≥ 0;
the half space y3 < 0 is given by the relations of Subsection B.1 under R∗ , which become
conditions at η = 0. On each rectangle of the partition each harmonic has degrees of its own
in σ and in η, or is absent, and the basis functions are joined across the rectangles to the
orders that Subsection B.1 prescribes. With this form the divergence vanishes identically, the
behaviour at the axis, at the origin and at infinity is that of the factors in (B.7) and (B.8),
and the symmetries hold.
   The equation is imposed in a weak form. We integrate (C.1) over a period and over R3
against fields of the same form on the same partition, with the exponent h = 3 in place of
h = 1. These test fields are divergence-free, so that the term ∇H drops out, and there are as
many of them as there are unknown coefficients. The products of harmonics in curl W × W
are formed on a grid in the two angles on which the harmonics depend, the integrals over each
rectangle are computed by Gauss quadrature, and one further equation fixes the translation
of the field in s; it is the equation that belongs to the unknown period. A computed field
is judged by its residual: the left-hand side of (C.1) without ∇H, computed pointwise from
the representation on a set of harmonics larger than that of the field, after subtraction of a
gradient fitted to it by least squares, and measured in the space H of Subsection 7.1. This
number approximates the norm in H of the left-hand side of (C.1) for the best choice of H,
and it steers the computation. The bounds (7.23) for the residuals (7.10) of a certificate are
among the conditions that the rigorous computation verifies.
   The discrete equations are solved by Newton’s method. The derivative of the equations with
respect to the coefficients is the weak form of the linearized operator
(C.2)          v 7→ ∂s v + 12 v + 12 (y · ∇)v + curl W × v + curl v × W + curl curl v
at the current field W ; its matrix is not formed, only applied to vectors. The linear system,
bordered by the derivative with respect to the period and by the equation that fixes the
translation in s, is solved by the flexible GMRES method. The preconditioner is a product
of block solves, where a block is the linearized operator restricted to a group of neighbouring
harmonics, in some blocks also to low degrees, and is factored by nested dissection over the
rectangles of the partition. Newton’s iteration is stopped after at most five steps.
C.3. Continuation and refinement. Newton’s method starts from a field that was reached
by continuation, in other discretizations than that of Subsection C.2: finite differences, and
74                        LEVENT ALPÖGE AND TRISTAN BUCKMASTER

then spectral elements. The continuation takes place in the family of equations
         ∂s U + (1 − g) U + g (y · ∇)U + (U · ∇)U + ∇π = ν ∆U + F,           div U = 0,
with a scaling parameter g, a viscosity ν > 0 and a force F ; the first two equations of (1.2)
are the case g = 21 , ν = 1, F = 0. It begins with an axisymmetric steady state for g near 0.502
and a small force, computed from a field whose swirl changes sign across the plane y3 = 0
and which is composed of a layer of fluid that flows inward along the plane, a column, and a
jet along the axis. On this layer we superpose waves of finite amplitude, with azimuthal wave
numbers from 14 to 20, whose dependence on time is a rigid rotation about the axis, and we
bring the force to zero, together with a damping term that this step introduces. We then pass
to the half space y3 > 0, with one family of waves and with the azimuthal wave number as a
real parameter. Without force the states so computed form two-parameter families, on which
g is a function of the parameters; on the first of these g > 21 everywhere, and the value g = 12
is reached on a second one. At g = 21 we superpose such a state and its image under R∗ , which
turns in the opposite sense, and we replace the condition at the plane y3 = 0 by the exact one.
Finally, through several refinements of the discretization, in which the viscosity takes the place
of g as an unknown, the wave number is brought to the integer 19, and the result is projected
onto a space of the form of Subsection C.2.
   From this starting field the approximate profile is computed in nine successive discretizations
of the form of Subsection C.2, each of which starts from the field of the one before, transferred
to the new degrees. In the first three the viscosity is an unknown beside the period, and a
normalization of the mean swirl is imposed: for the equations themselves, the solutions for
different viscosities are dilations of one another, and the normalization selects one of them.
The viscosity is then brought to one along this family. In the fourth discretization the viscosity
is one and the period is an unknown; the period found there is S̄, and it is kept in the five
discretizations that follow. The ninth field is W̄ .
   Between two discretizations the degrees are changed harmonic by harmonic and rectangle
by rectangle, by one of two rules. In the first, the contribution of each harmonic on each
rectangle to the residual, and the rate at which its coefficients decay with the degree, decide
whether its degrees are raised or lowered, and harmonics are added on the rectangles on which
the residual lies outside those that are present. In the second, the degrees are predicted from
the decay of the coefficients alone, each weighted by estimates of the local rates of the terms
of the equation. Far from the origin, where the radial phase has removed the oscillation of the
spiral arms, the degrees in the radius are tied to the frequencies in log |y| that remain, and the
degrees in η are the same in all rings.
C.4. Further fields of the certificate. The forcing fields bi and the fields Ξi of the con-
straints (7.11) are computed from the equation linearized about a field of the sequence of
Subsection C.3. On a space of perturbations coarser than that of the field, we solve the equa-
tion with the operator (C.2) for a given right-hand side, bordered as in Newton’s method by
the derivative with respect to the period and by a condition that removes the translation in
s. Perturbations are measured in a norm of the form of (7.14) with a constant weight w > 0,
and right-hand sides in H. A block Lanczos iteration gives the right-hand sides for which the
solution is largest relative to the right-hand side, with their solutions. Finitely many of these
pairs are kept, and to them is added the pair that accounts for the translations in time: the
left null vector of the bordered system and the derivative of the field in s. For each pair, the
forcing field bi is obtained from the right-hand side, and the constraint from the solution vi ,
                           BLOW-UP FOR THE NAVIER–STOKES EQUATIONS                                      75

through the field w vi − ∆vi : for a divergence-free field v,
                        ∇v, ∇(w vi − ∆vi ) H = h∆v, ∆vi iH + w h∇v, ∇vi iH
is the inner product of v and vi that belongs to this norm. Both fields are cut off in the radius
and smoothed, in such a way that they remain divergence-free and keep the symmetries. The
fields Φi and Ξi of Subsection B.1 are then produced on a mesh of cells of the meridian plane,
obtained by repeated division of a cell into four: Φi is, up to a normalization, r times a spline
fitted by least squares, whose coefficients are integer multiples of a power of 2, and Ξi is a
polynomial on each cell, with rounded coefficients, which approximates ∇Φi . This is why Ξi
is not a gradient, and why Subsection B.1 has the number cΞ .
    The fields W i of (B.9), 1 ≤ i ≤ n, with the vectors β i and the numbers ςi of Subsection B.1,
are computed approximate solutions of the linearized equation in which a combination of the
forcing fields and a change of the period are unknowns and the values of the constraints are
prescribed: with the field F (i) of (B.57), they solve approximately the equation F (i) ≃ 0. A
change of the period changes the radial phase, which is tied to the period. In these fields the
change is made only outside a ball, which is the origin of the function f∗ of (B.9), so that
inside the ball the approximate profiles are affine in the parameter. The adjoint fields ψj are
computed approximate solutions of the adjoint of the linearized equation whose right-hand
sides are combinations of the fields that represent the constraints, as (7.33) requires, with
prescribed pairings with the forcing fields, which (7.35) compares with the columns of the
matrix V ; they are sought with the exponent h = 3.
    The weights λp and µp of the multiplier (B.14) act on the harmonics outside the range
|ι2 | ≤ j∗ , |ι2 − ι1 | ≤ j∗ . In the computation the rates of rotation νp are, up to the sign, the
angular velocity 2π/(19 S̄) of the two patterns of Subsection C.1. In the frame that turns with
the first pattern the frequency (B.15) of the harmonic ι is 4πι2 /S̄, and in the frame that turns
with the second it is −4π(ι1 − ι2 )/S̄: outside the range each harmonic has, in one of the two
frames, a frequency of modulus larger than 4πj∗ /S̄. The multiplier turns this frequency into
positive terms, proportional to λp |Ωp (ι)| and to µp |Ωp (ι)|, in the coefficients of (B.16). Among
the errors that these terms have to absorb is the transport by W r + 21 y − νp ez × y, the velocity
relative to this frame; if this error alone is taken into account, without the derivatives of the
weight, the best weight at a point is proportional to the frequency divided by the square of
this speed. The weights are splines in the variables (B.3), and their coefficients are found by
a numerical optimization, which maximizes the smallest, over the cells and over the classes of
harmonics, of the differences between the two sides of the inequalities in which the weights
enter, each suitably normalized.
